<p><b>Charles Joseph Clark</b> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:85%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/King%27s_Privy_Council_for_Canada" title="King&#39;s Privy Council for Canada">PC</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Order_of_Canada" title="Order of Canada">CC</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Alberta_Order_of_Excellence" title="Alberta Order of Excellence">AOE</a></span> (born June 5, 1939) is a Canadian businessman, writer, and politician who served as the 16th <a href="/wiki/Prime_minister_of_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime minister of Canada">prime minister of Canada</a> from 1979 to 1980.
</p><p>Despite his relative inexperience, Clark rose quickly in federal politics, entering the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="House of Commons (Canada)">House of Commons</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1972_Canadian_federal_election" title="1972 Canadian federal election">1972 election</a> and winning the leadership of the <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_Party_of_Canada" title="Progressive Conservative Party of Canada">Progressive Conservative Party</a> in <a href="/wiki/1976_Progressive_Conservative_leadership_election" title="1976 Progressive Conservative leadership election">1976</a>. He won a <a href="/wiki/Minority_government" title="Minority government">minority government</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1979_Canadian_federal_election" title="1979 Canadian federal election">1979 election</a>, defeating the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada" title="Liberal Party of Canada">Liberal</a> government of <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Trudeau" title="Pierre Trudeau">Pierre Trudeau</a> and ending sixteen years of continuous Liberal rule. Taking office the day before his 40th birthday, Clark is the youngest person to become Prime Minister.
</p><p>Clark's tenure was brief as the minority government was brought down by a <a href="/wiki/Motion_of_no_confidence#Canada" title="Motion of no confidence">non-confidence vote</a> on his first budget in December 1979. The budget defeat triggered the <a href="/wiki/1980_Canadian_federal_election" title="1980 Canadian federal election">1980 election</a>. Clark and the Progressive Conservatives lost the election to Trudeau and the Liberals, who won a majority in the Commons and returned to power. Clark lost the leadership of the party to <a href="/wiki/Brian_Mulroney" title="Brian Mulroney">Brian Mulroney</a> in <a href="/wiki/1983_Progressive_Conservative_leadership_election" title="1983 Progressive Conservative leadership election">1983</a>.
</p><p>Clark returned to prominence from 1984 to 1993 as foreign minister then constitutional affairs minister in Mulroney's cabinet. Clark retired from politics by not standing for re-election for the House of Commons in <a href="/wiki/1993_Canadian_federal_election" title="1993 Canadian federal election">1993</a>. He made a political comeback in 1998 to lead the <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_Party_of_Canada" title="Progressive Conservative Party of Canada">Progressive Conservatives</a> in <a href="/wiki/2000_Canadian_federal_election" title="2000 Canadian federal election">their last general election</a> before the party's eventual dissolution, serving his final term in Parliament from 2000 to 2004. After the Progressive Conservatives merged with the more right-wing <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Alliance" title="Canadian Alliance">Canadian Alliance</a> to form the modern-day <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Canada" title="Conservative Party of Canada">Conservative Party of Canada</a> in 2003, Clark instead sat as an <a href="/wiki/Independent_politician" title="Independent politician">independent</a> Progressive Conservative, criticizing the merger as what he described as an "Alliance take-over", believing that the new party was drifting towards <a href="/wiki/Social_conservatism_in_Canada" title="Social conservatism in Canada">social conservatism</a>. Clark today serves as a university professor and as president of his own consulting firm.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_years">Early years</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Joe_Clark&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early years"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Charles_Clark_House.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/Charles_Clark_House.jpg/250px-Charles_Clark_House.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="167" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/Charles_Clark_House.jpg/375px-Charles_Clark_House.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/Charles_Clark_House.jpg/500px-Charles_Clark_House.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5182" data-file-height="3454" /></a><figcaption>Charles Clark House, a cultural heritage site in Canada, number 6563 in the Canadian Register of Historic Places<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>Charles Joseph Clark was born on June 5, 1939, in <a href="/wiki/High_River" title="High River">High River</a>, <a href="/wiki/Alberta" title="Alberta">Alberta</a>, the son of Grace Roselyn (née Welch) and local newspaper publisher Charles A. Clark.<sup id="cite_ref-Britannica_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Britannica-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Clark attended local schools and the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Alberta" title="University of Alberta">University of Alberta</a>, where he earned a bachelor's degree in history (1960) and a master's degree in <a href="/wiki/Political_science" title="Political science">political science</a> (1973).<sup id="cite_ref-Britannica_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Britannica-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> While in high school, he gained journalism experience with the <i>High River Times</i> and the <i><a href="/wiki/Calgary_Albertan" class="mw-redirect" title="Calgary Albertan">Calgary Albertan</a></i>. In his first year at the University of Alberta, Clark joined the staff of the campus newspaper, <i><a href="/wiki/The_Gateway_(newspaper)" class="mw-redirect" title="The Gateway (newspaper)">The Gateway</a></i>, and eventually became its editor-in-chief. Clark was also a member of the University of Alberta Debate Society (UADS). He later worked one summer at the <i><a href="/wiki/Edmonton_Journal" title="Edmonton Journal">Edmonton Journal</a></i> where he met his future biographer, <a href="/wiki/David_L._Humphreys" title="David L. Humphreys">David L. Humphreys</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Joe_Clark_1978_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Joe_Clark_1978-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Clark then attended <a href="/wiki/Schulich_School_of_Law" title="Schulich School of Law">Dalhousie Law School</a>.  However, he spent more time with the <a href="/wiki/Dalhousie_Student_Union" title="Dalhousie Student Union">Dalhousie Student Union</a>, Progressive Conservative politics and the <i><a href="/wiki/The_Dalhousie_Gazette" title="The Dalhousie Gazette">Dalhousie Gazette</a></i>, than on his courses.  After leaving Dalhousie, he unsuccessfully pursued first-year law studies at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_British_Columbia_Faculty_of_Law" class="mw-redirect" title="University of British Columbia Faculty of Law">University of British Columbia Faculty of Law</a> in Vancouver. He then worked full-time for the <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_Party_of_Canada" title="Progressive Conservative Party of Canada">Progressive Conservative Party</a>.
</p><p>In 1973, Clark married law student <a href="/wiki/Maureen_McTeer" title="Maureen McTeer">Maureen McTeer</a>. McTeer has developed her own career as a well-known author and lawyer and caused controversy by keeping her maiden name after marriage, a practice less common at the time.<sup id="cite_ref-Joe_Clark_1978_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Joe_Clark_1978-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Their daughter, <a href="/wiki/Catherine_Clark_(broadcaster)" title="Catherine Clark (broadcaster)">Catherine</a> has pursued a career in broadcasting.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_political_career">Early political career</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Joe_Clark&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Early political career"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Clark became politically active while at university, although he had been aware from a young age of politics in Canada. He competed with the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Alberta_Debate_Society" class="mw-redirect" title="University of Alberta Debate Society">University of Alberta Debate Society</a>. He served as president of the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Alberta" title="University of Alberta">University of Alberta</a> Young Progressive Conservatives and eventually served as national president for the Young PCs group.<sup id="cite_ref-Mulroney_1991_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Mulroney_1991-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> Clark sparred with future political rival <a href="/wiki/Preston_Manning" title="Preston Manning">Preston Manning</a> in debate forums on campus between the Young PCs and the Youth League of the <a href="/wiki/Alberta_Social_Credit_Party" title="Alberta Social Credit Party">Alberta Social Credit Party</a>. Clark encountered another future rival when he met <a href="/wiki/Brian_Mulroney" title="Brian Mulroney">Brian Mulroney</a> at a national Young PCs meeting in 1958.<sup id="cite_ref-Mulroney_1991_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Mulroney_1991-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Clark spent time in France to improve his fluency in the French language and took courses in French while he was living in <a href="/wiki/Ottawa" title="Ottawa">Ottawa</a>. He eventually became comfortable speaking and answering questions in French.<sup id="cite_ref-Joe_Clark_1978_5-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Joe_Clark_1978-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Clark entered politics at age 28 but was unsuccessful as candidate for the provincial <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_Association_of_Alberta" title="Progressive Conservative Association of Alberta">Progressive Conservatives</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1967_Alberta_general_election" title="1967 Alberta general election">1967 provincial election</a>. He served as a chief assistant to provincial opposition leader and future Premier <a href="/wiki/Peter_Lougheed" title="Peter Lougheed">Peter Lougheed</a> and served in the office of federal opposition leader <a href="/wiki/Robert_Stanfield" title="Robert Stanfield">Robert Stanfield</a>, learning the inner workings of Parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-Mulroney_1991_7-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Mulroney_1991-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> Clark unsuccessfully ran for the <a href="/wiki/Legislative_Assembly_of_Alberta" title="Legislative Assembly of Alberta">Legislative Assembly of Alberta</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1971_Alberta_general_election" title="1971 Alberta general election">1971 provincial election</a>. He then successfully ran in the federal election <a href="/wiki/1972_Canadian_federal_election" title="1972 Canadian federal election">held a year later</a> and was elected to Parliament as the MP for <a href="/wiki/Rocky_Mountain_(federal_electoral_district)" title="Rocky Mountain (federal electoral district)">Rocky Mountain</a>, a largely rural riding in southwestern Alberta.
</p><p>Clark had initially been viewed with suspicion, but over time won over senior members of the party. Robert Stanfield initially thought Clark not to be fit for politics, but over time came to view him as leadership material. Erik Nielsen viewed Clark as fiercely partisan, but was impressed by Clark's questioning the government.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Clark's social liberalism put him at odds with the right-wing members of his caucus, several of whom were not afraid to confront him. For example, in the lead-up to the 1979 election, the bulk of Clark's riding was merged into the newly created riding of <a href="/wiki/Bow_River_(electoral_district)" title="Bow River (electoral district)">Bow River</a> during a <a href="/wiki/Redistribution_(election)" title="Redistribution (election)">redistribution</a> of ridings.  Fellow Tory MP <a href="/wiki/Stanley_Schumacher" title="Stanley Schumacher">Stanley Schumacher</a> had much of his old riding of <a href="/wiki/Palliser_(Alberta_electoral_district)" title="Palliser (Alberta electoral district)">Palliser</a> merged into Bow River as well.  Even though Clark was now party leader, Schumacher refused to step aside in Clark's favour, forcing Clark to run in nearby <a href="/wiki/Yellowhead_(electoral_district)" title="Yellowhead (electoral district)">Yellowhead</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Mulroney_1991_7-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Mulroney_1991-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Progressive_Conservative_leadership_convention.2C_1976"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Progressive_Conservative_leadership_convention,_1976">Progressive Conservative leadership convention, 1976</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Joe_Clark&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Progressive Conservative leadership convention, 1976"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Following the resignation of PC party leader <a href="/wiki/Robert_Stanfield" title="Robert Stanfield">Robert Stanfield</a>, Clark sought and won the leadership of the PC Party at the <a href="/wiki/1976_Progressive_Conservative_leadership_election" title="1976 Progressive Conservative leadership election">1976 leadership convention</a>. Initially, the favourite among <a href="/wiki/Red_Tory" title="Red Tory">Red Tories</a> was <a href="/wiki/Flora_MacDonald_(politician)" title="Flora MacDonald (politician)">Flora MacDonald</a>; however, she did worse than expected, while Clark placed a surprising third in a field of eleven on the first ballot of convention delegates, behind only <a href="/wiki/Claude_Wagner" title="Claude Wagner">Claude Wagner</a> and <a href="/wiki/Brian_Mulroney" title="Brian Mulroney">Brian Mulroney</a>. MacDonald dropped off after the second ballot, encouraging her supporters to support Clark, who quickly became the compromise Red Tory candidate. The party's right-wing rallied behind Wagner. Mulroney, a <a href="/wiki/Quebec" title="Quebec">Quebec</a> businessman with no elected political experience, was unable to expand his base of support significantly. As other Red Tory candidates were eliminated during the first four ballots, Clark gradually overtook Mulroney and then Wagner to emerge as the victor on the fourth ballot, by 1,187 votes to 1,122.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Clark, who won the Tory leadership at age 36, remains the youngest leader of a major federal party in the history of Canadian politics. With many veteran Tories having been defeated in the 1968 election, the party effectively skipped a generation by selecting Clark as its new leader.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Opposition_Leader.2C_1976.E2.80.931979"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Opposition_Leader,_1976–1979">Opposition Leader, 1976–1979</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Joe_Clark&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Opposition Leader, 1976–1979"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Joe Clark's rapid rise from a relatively unknown Alberta MP to the <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Official_Opposition_(Canada)" title="Leader of the Official Opposition (Canada)">Leader of the Opposition</a> took much of Canada by surprise. <i><a href="/wiki/Toronto_Star" title="Toronto Star">The Toronto Star</a></i> announced Clark's victory with a headline that read "Joe Who?", giving Clark a nickname that stuck for years. His clumsiness and awkward mannerisms were mocked by some political commentators, such as cartoonist <a href="/wiki/Andy_Donato" title="Andy Donato">Andy Donato</a> who typically drew Clark with mittens on strings hanging from his suit sleeves.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>However, Clark hired experienced staffers such as <a href="/wiki/Lowell_Murray" title="Lowell Murray">Lowell Murray</a>, <a href="/wiki/Duncan_Edmonds" title="Duncan Edmonds">Duncan Edmonds</a>, and William Neville, who shaped his policies and ran his office. He improved his party's standing in national opinion polls. Clark gradually earned the respect of some political observers, including his own caucus, and benefited when live television came to the House of Commons in 1977.<sup id="cite_ref-Joe_Clark_1978_5-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Joe_Clark_1978-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Some observers noted that Clark, despite being perceived by many people as something of a square, showed biting wit at times while in Opposition. One of his most famous quips was: "A recession is when your neighbour loses his job. A depression is when you lose your job. Recovery is when Pierre Trudeau loses his job."<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;nb 1&#93;</a></sup> Television broadcasts of moments from Prime Minister questions served to build Clark and the Tories up as an alternative to the Liberals.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1979_election">1979 election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Joe_Clark&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: 1979 election"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Joe_Clark_May_18_1979.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/Joe_Clark_May_18_1979.jpg/220px-Joe_Clark_May_18_1979.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="285" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/Joe_Clark_May_18_1979.jpg/330px-Joe_Clark_May_18_1979.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/Joe_Clark_May_18_1979.jpg/440px-Joe_Clark_May_18_1979.jpg 2x" data-file-width="735" data-file-height="951" /></a><figcaption>Clark in 1979</figcaption></figure>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1097763485"><table class="box-BLP_unsourced_section plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content ambox-BLP_unsourced" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><span typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/b4/Ambox_important.svg/40px-Ambox_important.svg.png" decoding="async" width="40" height="40" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/b4/Ambox_important.svg/60px-Ambox_important.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/b4/Ambox_important.svg/80px-Ambox_important.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="40" data-file-height="40" /></span></span></div></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This section of a <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Biographies_of_living_persons" title="Wikipedia:Biographies of living persons">biography of a living person</a> <b>does not <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources">include</a> any <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">references or sources</a></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please help by adding <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Reliable_sources" title="Wikipedia:Reliable sources">reliable sources</a>. Contentious material about living people that is unsourced or poorly sourced <b>must be removed immediately</b>.<br /><small><span class="plainlinks"><i>Find sources:</i>&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?as_eq=wikipedia&amp;q=%22Joe+Clark%22">"Joe Clark"</a>&#160;–&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?tbm=nws&amp;q=%22Joe+Clark%22+-wikipedia&amp;tbs=ar:1">news</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?&amp;q=%22Joe+Clark%22&amp;tbs=bkt:s&amp;tbm=bks">newspapers</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?tbs=bks:1&amp;q=%22Joe+Clark%22+-wikipedia">books</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://scholar.google.com/scholar?q=%22Joe+Clark%22">scholar</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.jstor.org/action/doBasicSearch?Query=%22Joe+Clark%22&amp;acc=on&amp;wc=on">JSTOR</a></span></small></span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">April 2020</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Large budget deficits, high inflation, and high unemployment made the Liberal government unpopular. Trudeau had put off asking the Canadian <a href="/wiki/Governor_General" class="mw-redirect" title="Governor General">Governor General</a> to <a href="/wiki/1979_Canadian_federal_election" title="1979 Canadian federal election">call an election</a> as long as possible, in the hope that his party could recover popular support but it backfired, as there was growing public antipathy towards his perceived arrogance. Clark campaigned on the slogans, "Let's get Canada working again", and "It's time for a change—give the future a chance!"
</p><p>In the latter half of the campaign, the Liberals focused their attacks on Clark's perceived inexperience. Their advertisements declared "This is no time for on-the-job training", and "We need tough leadership to keep Canada growing. A leader must be a leader." Clark played into their hands by appearing bumbling and unsure in public.
</p><p>When Clark undertook a tour of the Middle East in order to show his ability to handle foreign affairs issues, his luggage was lost, and Clark appeared to be uncomfortable with the issues being discussed. That incident was widely lampooned by <a href="/wiki/Toronto_Sun" title="Toronto Sun">Toronto Sun</a> cartoonist <a href="/wiki/Andy_Donato" title="Andy Donato">Andy Donato</a>. During the same tour, while inspecting a military <a href="/wiki/Honour_guard" class="mw-redirect" title="Honour guard">honour guard</a>, Clark turned too soon and nearly bumped into a soldier's <a href="/wiki/Bayonet" title="Bayonet">bayonet</a>; one of the first major media reports on the incident claimed, with some exaggeration, that he had nearly been beheaded.
</p><p>Despite Clark being bilingual, the Tories were unable to make much headway in Quebec, which continued to be federally dominated by the Liberals. While Clark's 1976 leadership rivals were prominent in that province, <a href="/wiki/Claude_Wagner" title="Claude Wagner">Claude Wagner</a> had left politics (he died shortly after the election), while <a href="/wiki/Brian_Mulroney" title="Brian Mulroney">Brian Mulroney</a> was still bitter about his loss and turned down an offer to serve under Clark.
</p><p>Nonetheless, Clark's Progressive Conservatives won 136 seats to end sixteen continuous years of Liberal rule in the election for the <a href="/wiki/31st_Canadian_Parliament" title="31st Canadian Parliament">31st Canadian Parliament</a>. Despite receiving fewer votes than the Liberals nationally, the Progressive Conservatives won the popular vote in seven provinces.  They also made  gains in Ontario, particularly in the Toronto suburbs, winning many seats by narrow margins, offsetting a large Liberal win in Quebec.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> The Tories were only able to win two seats in Quebec, leaving them six seats short of a majority.  The Liberals lost 27 seats, including several high-profile cabinet ministers, and Trudeau announced his intention to step down as party leader.
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_minister_.281979.E2.80.931980.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_minister_(1979–1980)">Prime minister (1979–1980)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Joe_Clark&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Prime minister (1979–1980)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>On June 4, 1979, the day before his 40th birthday, Clark was sworn in as Canada's 16th prime minister, steering the first Tory government since the defeat of <a href="/wiki/John_Diefenbaker" title="John Diefenbaker">John Diefenbaker</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1963_Canadian_federal_election" title="1963 Canadian federal election">1963 election</a>. He was Canada's youngest Prime Minister, and the first to be born in Western Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>With a <a href="/wiki/Minority_government" title="Minority government">minority government</a> in the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_Canada" title="House of Commons of Canada">House of Commons</a>, Clark had to rely on the support of the <a href="/wiki/Social_Credit_Party_of_Canada" title="Social Credit Party of Canada">Social Credit Party</a>, with its six seats, or the <a href="/wiki/New_Democratic_Party_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="New Democratic Party (Canada)">New Democratic Party</a> (NDP), with its 26 seats. At the time, <a href="/wiki/Official_Opposition_(Canada)" title="Official Opposition (Canada)">Opposition</a> leader Trudeau said that he would allow the Progressive Conservatives a chance to govern, though he warned the Prime Minister against dismantling <a href="/wiki/Petro-Canada" title="Petro-Canada">Petro-Canada</a>, which was unpopular in Clark's home province of Alberta.<sup id="cite_ref-archives.cbc.ca_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-archives.cbc.ca-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Social Credit was below the 12 seats needed for <a href="/wiki/Official_party_status" title="Official party status">official party status</a> in the House of Commons. However, the six seats would have been just enough to give Clark's government a majority had the Progressive Conservatives formed a <a href="/wiki/Coalition_government" title="Coalition government">coalition government</a> with Social Credit, or had the two parties otherwise agreed to work together. Clark managed to lure Socred MP <a href="/wiki/Richard_Janelle" title="Richard Janelle">Richard Janelle</a> to the government caucus, but this still left the Tories five seats short of a majority. Clark however decided that he would govern as if he had a majority,<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> and refused to grant the small Socred official party status, form a coalition, or co-operate with the party in any way.
</p><p>Clark was unable to accomplish much in office because of the tenuous situation of his minority government. However, historians have credited Clark's government with making <a href="/wiki/Access_to_information" class="mw-redirect" title="Access to information">access to information</a> legislation a priority.<sup id="cite_ref-infocom.gc.ca_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-infocom.gc.ca-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> The Clark government introduced Bill C-15, the <i><a href="/wiki/Freedom_of_information_in_Canada" title="Freedom of information in Canada">Freedom of Information Act</a></i>, which established a broad right of access to government records, an elaborate scheme of exemptions, and a two-stage review process. The legislation was debated at <a href="/wiki/Second_reading" class="mw-redirect" title="Second reading">second reading</a> at the end of November 1979 and was referred to the Standing Committee on Justice and Legal Affairs. Within days the minority Conservative government was unseated; the legislation died on the order paper.<sup id="cite_ref-infocom.gc.ca_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-infocom.gc.ca-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> The re-elected Trudeau government subsequently based its <i><a href="/wiki/Access_to_Information_Act" title="Access to Information Act">Access to Information Act</a></i> on the Clark government's Bill C-15. The <i>Access to Information Act</i> received royal assent in July 1982 and came into force in July 1983.<sup id="cite_ref-infocom.gc.ca_20-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-infocom.gc.ca-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> The public now has the legal right of access to government records in some 150 federal departments and agencies.<sup id="cite_ref-infocom.gc.ca_20-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-infocom.gc.ca-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Though the election had been held in May, Parliament did not resume sitting until October, one of the longest break periods in Confederation.<sup id="cite_ref-archives.cbc.ca_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-archives.cbc.ca-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> The gas tax in the budget soured Clark's relationship with Ontario Premier <a href="/wiki/Bill_Davis" title="Bill Davis">Bill Davis</a>,<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> even though both were Red Tories. 
</p><p>During the campaign, Clark committed to move Canada's embassy in <a href="/wiki/Israel" title="Israel">Israel</a> from <a href="/wiki/Tel_Aviv" title="Tel Aviv">Tel Aviv</a> to <a href="/wiki/Jerusalem" title="Jerusalem">Jerusalem</a>, and re-affirmed this promise shortly after taking office.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The move proved somewhat contentious within the cabinet, in part due to economic concerns that might have come about from the move.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Moreover, it proved a perilous situation as moving the embassy risked a negative economic response, or a violent one from terrorists, and not doing so would make Clark look indecisive.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Ultimately, the Cark government backed off from doing so on October 29, 1979, until Jerusalem's status could be clarified with Israel's neighbors.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_21-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Internationally, Clark represented Canada in June 1979 at the <a href="/wiki/5th_G7_summit" title="5th G7 summit">5th G7 summit</a> in Tokyo.<sup id="cite_ref-eu_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-eu-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> Clark reportedly had a good relationship with US President <a href="/wiki/Jimmy_Carter" title="Jimmy Carter">Jimmy Carter</a>, who phoned Clark to thank him personally for his role in the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Caper" title="Canadian Caper">Canadian Caper</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Caper_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Caper-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Fall_of_government">Fall of government</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Joe_Clark&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Fall of government"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>During the 1979 election campaign, Clark had promised to cut taxes to stimulate the economy. However, once in office, the <a href="/wiki/1979_Canadian_federal_budget" title="1979 Canadian federal budget">1979 budget</a> he proposed was designed to curb inflation by slowing economic activity. The budget also proposed a 4-cent per litre (18-cent per <a href="/wiki/Gallon" title="Gallon">gallon</a>) tax on gasoline in order to reduce the budgetary deficit.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Finance Minister <a href="/wiki/John_Crosbie" title="John Crosbie">John Crosbie</a> touted the budget as "short term pain for long term gain".<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Though Clark had hoped this change in policy would work to his advantage, it actually earned him widespread animosity as a politician who could not keep his promises, even in such a short period.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Clark's refusal to work with the Socreds, combined with the gasoline tax, came back to haunt him when the budget came before the House of Commons in December 1979.  On December 12, NDP Finance Critic <a href="/wiki/Bob_Rae" title="Bob Rae">Bob Rae</a> proposed a subamendment to the budget motion, stating that the House of Commons did not approve of the budget.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> The Liberals supported the NDP subamendment. The five Socred MPs had demanded the gas tax revenues be allocated to Quebec and abstained when Clark turned them down. On December 13, the subamendment passed on a 139–133 margin.<sup id="cite_ref-archives.cbc.ca_18-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-archives.cbc.ca-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Clark was criticized for his "inability to do math" in failing to predict the outcome, not only because he was in a minority situation, but also because three members of his caucus would be absent for the crucial budget vote. One was ill and two were stuck abroad on official business. The Liberals by contrast had assembled their entire caucus, save one, for the occasion.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1980_election">1980 election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Joe_Clark&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: 1980 election"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The vote on Rae's amendment was partially welcomed by Clark and the Tories.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (March 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Dates_and_numbers#Chronological_items" title="Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Dates and numbers"><span title="The time period mentioned near this tag is ambiguous. (March 2022)">when?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> When a new election was called, Clark expected his party would be able to defeat the demoralized and leaderless Liberals easily, since Trudeau had announced his intention to step aside and the Liberals had yet to hold a leadership convention. However, the Progressive Conservatives had misjudged the electorate, since they had not commissioned any polls since August. A November Gallup poll published eight days before the December 11 budget reported that their popularity was down from 36% during the summer to 28%, with the party 19 points behind the Liberals, giving the latter the popular support to initiate the non-confidence motion.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> After the government fell, Clark's party was caught off guard when Pierre Trudeau quickly rescinded his resignation from the Liberal leadership to lead his party into the subsequent election.
</p><p>Clark's Tories campaigned under the slogan, "Real change deserves a fair chance."<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> Trudeau swept the Liberals back into power in the February <a href="/wiki/1980_Canadian_federal_election" title="1980 Canadian federal election">1980 election</a> with 147 seats, against 103 for the Progressive Conservatives. Davis' criticism of the gas tax was used in the Liberals' <a href="/wiki/Ontario" title="Ontario">Ontario</a> television ads.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> The Tories lost 19 seats in that province, which ultimately proved to be decisive in the campaign.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Clark's government would last a total of nine months less a day.  As Clark's Finance Minister, John Crosbie, famously described it in his own inimitable way: "Long enough to conceive, just not long enough to deliver."<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Supreme_Court_appointments">Supreme Court appointments</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Joe_Clark&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Supreme Court appointments"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Clark chose <a href="/wiki/Julien_Chouinard" title="Julien Chouinard">Julien Chouinard</a> to be appointed as a justice of the <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Canada" title="Supreme Court of Canada">Supreme Court of Canada</a> by the <a href="/wiki/Governor_General_of_Canada" title="Governor General of Canada">Governor General</a>, who served from September 24, 1979, to February 6, 1987.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Relationship_between_Trudeau_and_Clark">Relationship between Trudeau and Clark</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Joe_Clark&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Relationship between Trudeau and Clark"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Trudeau commented in his memoirs, published in 1993, that Clark was much more tough and aggressive than past Tory leader <a href="/wiki/Robert_Stanfield" title="Robert Stanfield">Robert Stanfield</a>, noting that those qualities served Clark well in his party winning the 1979 election victory. Trudeau also complimented Clark as a respectable leader and a better choice over <a href="/wiki/Brian_Mulroney" title="Brian Mulroney">Brian Mulroney</a>, who had defeated Clark at the <a href="/wiki/1983_Progressive_Conservative_leadership_election" title="1983 Progressive Conservative leadership election">leadership convention</a> in 1983. Trudeau told his friends that the Tories had chosen the wrong man.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Opposition_leader.2C_1980.E2.80.931983"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Opposition_leader,_1980–1983">Opposition leader, 1980–1983</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Joe_Clark&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Opposition leader, 1980–1983"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Opposition to Clark's leadership began to grow after the fall of the PC minority government, and the party's defeat by a resurgent Liberal Party. There were frequent rumors that several potential challengers were covertly undermining Clark's leadership.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Though in 1982 <a href="/wiki/Brian_Mulroney" title="Brian Mulroney">Brian Mulroney</a> deliberately appeared at a press conference with Clark to say that he was not seeking the leadership of the PC party, he was in fact conspiring to oust Clark.<sup id="cite_ref-gutsy_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-gutsy-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Liberal Party had regained national prominence by leading the "No" side to victory in the <a href="/wiki/1980_Quebec_referendum" title="1980 Quebec referendum">1980 Quebec referendum</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Patriation_Reference" title="Patriation Reference">Constitution patriation</a>. While Trudeau's <a href="/wiki/National_Energy_Program" title="National Energy Program">National Energy Program</a> was hugely unpopular in Western Canada, especially <a href="/wiki/Alberta" title="Alberta">Alberta</a>, it was able to shore up Liberal support in the voter-rich Eastern Canada, particularly <a href="/wiki/Ontario" title="Ontario">Ontario</a> and <a href="/wiki/Quebec" title="Quebec">Quebec</a>, generally having the opposite effect of Clark's proposed gas tax.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Difficult budgets and the economic recession resulted in Trudeau's approval ratings declining after the bounce from the 1982 Constitution patriation and showed his party headed for certain defeat by early 1984, prompting him to retire.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> However, Clark was unable to stay on as Progressive Conservative leader long enough to regain the Prime Ministership.
</p><p>On February 28, 1981, during the party's national convention, 33.5% of the delegates supported a <a href="/wiki/Leadership_review" title="Leadership review">leadership review</a>; they felt that Clark would not be able to lead the party to victory again but Clark considered two thirds of delegates voting no to be an endorsement.<sup id="cite_ref-gutsy_34-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-gutsy-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> At the January 1983 convention in <a href="/wiki/Winnipeg" title="Winnipeg">Winnipeg</a>, 33.1% supported a review.<sup id="cite_ref-fought_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fought-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> This was also considering that the governing <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada" title="Liberal Party of Canada">Liberals</a> under <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Trudeau" title="Pierre Trudeau">Pierre Trudeau</a> were slipping in polls, and although the PCs had built up a substantial lead in popularity, Trudeau was expected to retire before the election and a new Liberal leader could have been able to pull off a victory. Moreover, some in the party felt Clark to be too liberal and would be a liability come election time.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1983_leadership_convention">1983 leadership convention</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Joe_Clark&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: 1983 leadership convention"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1983, after declaring that an endorsement by 66.9% of delegates at the party's biennial convention was not enough, Clark called a <a href="/wiki/1983_Progressive_Conservative_leadership_election" title="1983 Progressive Conservative leadership election">leadership convention</a> to decide the issue. (In December 2007, German-Canadian businessman and lobbyist <a href="/wiki/Karlheinz_Schreiber" title="Karlheinz Schreiber">Karlheinz Schreiber</a> told the House of Commons Ethics Committee that he and other Germans, including <a href="/wiki/Bavaria" title="Bavaria">Bavarian</a> politician <a href="/wiki/Franz_Josef_Strauss" title="Franz Josef Strauss">Franz Josef Strauss</a>, and Austrian-Canadian entrepreneur <a href="/wiki/Walter_Wolf" title="Walter Wolf">Walter Wolf</a>, had contributed significant funds to finance <a href="/wiki/Quebec" title="Quebec">Quebec</a> delegates to vote against Clark at Winnipeg, denying him the mandate he sought. A <a href="/wiki/Public_inquiry" title="Public inquiry">public inquiry</a> on these matters, and on other business dealings between Mulroney and Schreiber, was called for early 2008 by Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Stephen_Harper" title="Stephen Harper">Stephen Harper</a>. This led further to the 2009 <a href="/wiki/Oliphant_Commission" class="mw-redirect" title="Oliphant Commission">Oliphant Commission</a>.
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Joe_Clark_PC_LeadershipConvention_1983.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Joe_Clark_PC_LeadershipConvention_1983.jpg/275px-Joe_Clark_PC_LeadershipConvention_1983.jpg" decoding="async" width="275" height="413" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Joe_Clark_PC_LeadershipConvention_1983.jpg/413px-Joe_Clark_PC_LeadershipConvention_1983.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Joe_Clark_PC_LeadershipConvention_1983.jpg/550px-Joe_Clark_PC_LeadershipConvention_1983.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1500" data-file-height="2250" /></a><figcaption>Joe Clark on the floor of the 1983 leadership convention.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Clark immediately nominated to keep his leader's post, and retained support from most of the Red Tories and other party members who were opposed to the public attacks on his leadership by others in the party.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Clark already had most of a campaign team up and running by the time he called the leadership convention, as he had mobilized support to help gain in the convention's leadership review.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> However, Mulroney and <a href="/wiki/John_Crosbie" title="John Crosbie">John Crosbie</a> had been laying the groundwork for a campaign for some time, with Crosbie expecting Clark to lose or resign soon, and Mulroney supportive of the anti-Clark movement.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>In a rematch of the 1976 convention, Mulroney emerged as the main challenger, gaining the support of the party's right wing, which viewed Clark as too progressive and opposed his continued leadership.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Other party members felt that the federal Liberal Party's stranglehold on Quebec seats (they held all but one of the province's 75 seats) could only be broken by a native from that province, which gave Mulroney considerable support. Media coverage emphasized the pro-business and <a href="/wiki/Neoliberalism" title="Neoliberalism">neo-liberal</a> bent of most of the candidates as a "Changing of the Guard" within the PC party from their more classical conservative and moderate elements. Clark's campaign countered this by trying to polarize the election between right wingers and a centrist who had been able to win before. The Mulroney campaign responded by continuing their pro-business line.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Several candidates agreed to an "ABC" (Anybody But Clark) strategy for the convention and when news of that back-room deal broke out, support was expected to rally around the party's embattled leader.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> During delegate voting, Clark won the first ballot, but only won 36.5% of the vote, well short of the 50% required. His support dwindled over the next two ballots. Mulroney, who was endorsed by all but two candidates, defeated Clark on the fourth ballot. Clark urged his supporters to leave the convention united behind Mulroney,<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> and agreed to serve under him.
</p><p>Many political observers and analysts have questioned Clark's rationale for the decision. One famous incident involved a 1987 official dinner held for <a href="/wiki/Charles,_Prince_of_Wales" class="mw-redirect" title="Charles, Prince of Wales">Prince Charles</a> at <a href="/wiki/Rideau_Hall" title="Rideau Hall">Rideau Hall</a>. When the Prince met Clark in the receiving line at the function, he asked to Clark: "why wasn't two thirds enough?"<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> Clark's wife, <a href="/wiki/Maureen_McTeer" title="Maureen McTeer">Maureen McTeer</a>, elaborated on Clark's decision in her 2003 autobiography, <i>In My Own Name</i>. McTeer suggested that for her husband, anything less than a 75% endorsement would not have been a clear enough mandate to forge onwards from the party membership. Clark feared that the 34% of PC members who did not support him would become his most vocal critics in the upcoming election campaign, and that his continued leadership would have led to fractures in the party.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Clark was convinced that he could win another leadership race and gain a clear level of support, once his qualities were compared against the handful of politically inexperienced challengers who coveted his position and who were covertly undermining his leadership.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Member_of_Mulroney_cabinet">Member of Mulroney cabinet</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Joe_Clark&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Member of Mulroney cabinet"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span id="Secretary_of_State_for_External_Affairs_.28Minister_of_Foreign_Affairs.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Secretary_of_State_for_External_Affairs_(Minister_of_Foreign_Affairs)">Secretary of State for External Affairs (Minister of Foreign Affairs)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Joe_Clark&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Secretary of State for External Affairs (Minister of Foreign Affairs)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1097763485"><table class="box-BLP_unsourced_section plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content ambox-BLP_unsourced" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><span typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/b4/Ambox_important.svg/40px-Ambox_important.svg.png" decoding="async" width="40" height="40" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/b4/Ambox_important.svg/60px-Ambox_important.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/b4/Ambox_important.svg/80px-Ambox_important.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="40" data-file-height="40" /></span></span></div></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This section of a <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Biographies_of_living_persons" title="Wikipedia:Biographies of living persons">biography of a living person</a> <b>does not <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources">include</a> any <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">references or sources</a></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please help by adding <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Reliable_sources" title="Wikipedia:Reliable sources">reliable sources</a>. Contentious material about living people that is unsourced or poorly sourced <b>must be removed immediately</b>.<br /><small><span class="plainlinks"><i>Find sources:</i>&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?as_eq=wikipedia&amp;q=%22Joe+Clark%22">"Joe Clark"</a>&#160;–&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?tbm=nws&amp;q=%22Joe+Clark%22+-wikipedia&amp;tbs=ar:1">news</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?&amp;q=%22Joe+Clark%22&amp;tbs=bkt:s&amp;tbm=bks">newspapers</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?tbs=bks:1&amp;q=%22Joe+Clark%22+-wikipedia">books</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://scholar.google.com/scholar?q=%22Joe+Clark%22">scholar</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.jstor.org/action/doBasicSearch?Query=%22Joe+Clark%22&amp;acc=on&amp;wc=on">JSTOR</a></span></small></span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">April 2020</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>The Progressive Conservatives, led by Mulroney, went on to win a huge victory in the <a href="/wiki/1984_Canadian_federal_election" title="1984 Canadian federal election">1984 election</a>, and Mulroney became prime minister.
</p><p>Despite their personal differences, Clark ably served in Mulroney's cabinet as the Secretary of State for External Affairs, as the <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Foreign_Affairs_(Canada)" title="Minister of Foreign Affairs (Canada)">Minister of Foreign Affairs</a> was known before 1993.
</p><p>Some of Clark's accomplishments and bold moves in this role included:
</p>
<ul><li>convincing Mulroney to recommend the appointment of <a href="/wiki/Stephen_Lewis" title="Stephen Lewis">Stephen Lewis</a> as Canada's ambassador to the United Nations – who later became the UN special envoy on the <a href="/wiki/HIV/AIDS" title="HIV/AIDS">AIDS</a> crisis; many believe Lewis' appointment was Clark's price to serve under Mulroney;</li>
<li>in 1984, being the very first <a href="/wiki/Developed_nation" class="mw-redirect" title="Developed nation">developed nation</a> foreign affairs minister to land in previously isolated <a href="/wiki/Ethiopia" title="Ethiopia">Ethiopia</a> to lead the Western response to the <a href="/wiki/1983%E2%80%931985_famine_in_Ethiopia" title="1983–1985 famine in Ethiopia">1983–1985 famine in Ethiopia</a>; Canada's response was overwhelming, and led the United States and Great Britain to follow suit almost immediately – an unprecedented situation in foreign affairs to that time, since Ethiopia had a <a href="/wiki/Marxism" title="Marxism">Marxist</a> one-party state and had previously been wholly isolated by "the West";</li>
<li>taking a strong stand against <a href="/wiki/Apartheid_in_South_Africa" class="mw-redirect" title="Apartheid in South Africa">apartheid</a> and for <a href="/wiki/Economic_sanction" class="mw-redirect" title="Economic sanction">economic sanctions</a> against South Africa at a time when Canadian allies <a href="/wiki/Ronald_Reagan" title="Ronald Reagan">Ronald Reagan</a> and <a href="/wiki/Margaret_Thatcher" title="Margaret Thatcher">Margaret Thatcher</a> opposed such sanctions;</li>
<li>taking a strong stand against American intervention in <a href="/wiki/Nicaragua" title="Nicaragua">Nicaragua</a>;</li>
<li>accepting refugees from <a href="/wiki/El_Salvador" title="El Salvador">El Salvador</a> and <a href="/wiki/Guatemala" title="Guatemala">Guatemala</a>;</li>
<li>managing nonetheless to maintain extremely strong ties with the US, helping steer the <a href="/wiki/North_American_Free_Trade_Agreement" title="North American Free Trade Agreement">North American Free Trade Agreement</a> negotiations to a final agreement.</li></ul>
<p>During his term as External Affairs minister, Clark championed Canada's unabashed disapproval of the apartheid regime in South Africa. Canada was the only <a href="/wiki/G7" title="G7">G7</a> nation to take such a resolute stance against the apartheid regime during the 1980s. He also took on the difficult Constitution ministerial portfolio after the failure of the <a href="/wiki/Meech_Lake_Accord" title="Meech Lake Accord">Meech Lake Accord</a>, and vigorously pursued his task.
</p><p>He maintained Canada's independent voice politically and socially at a time of increasing economic integration with the US and the rise of more socially conservative <a href="/wiki/Right-wing_politics" title="Right-wing politics">right-wing politics</a> there.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Minister_responsible_for_Constitutional_Affairs">Minister responsible for Constitutional Affairs</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Joe_Clark&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Minister responsible for Constitutional Affairs"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1097763485"><table class="box-BLP_unsourced_section plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content ambox-BLP_unsourced" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><span typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/b4/Ambox_important.svg/40px-Ambox_important.svg.png" decoding="async" width="40" height="40" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/b4/Ambox_important.svg/60px-Ambox_important.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/b4/Ambox_important.svg/80px-Ambox_important.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="40" data-file-height="40" /></span></span></div></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This section of a <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Biographies_of_living_persons" title="Wikipedia:Biographies of living persons">biography of a living person</a> <b>does not <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources">include</a> any <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">references or sources</a></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please help by adding <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Reliable_sources" title="Wikipedia:Reliable sources">reliable sources</a>. Contentious material about living people that is unsourced or poorly sourced <b>must be removed immediately</b>.<br /><small><span class="plainlinks"><i>Find sources:</i>&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?as_eq=wikipedia&amp;q=%22Joe+Clark%22">"Joe Clark"</a>&#160;–&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?tbm=nws&amp;q=%22Joe+Clark%22+-wikipedia&amp;tbs=ar:1">news</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?&amp;q=%22Joe+Clark%22&amp;tbs=bkt:s&amp;tbm=bks">newspapers</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?tbs=bks:1&amp;q=%22Joe+Clark%22+-wikipedia">books</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://scholar.google.com/scholar?q=%22Joe+Clark%22">scholar</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.jstor.org/action/doBasicSearch?Query=%22Joe+Clark%22&amp;acc=on&amp;wc=on">JSTOR</a></span></small></span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">April 2020</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Clark later served as the <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_Queen%27s_Privy_Council_for_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="President of the Queen&#39;s Privy Council for Canada">President of the Queen's Privy Council for Canada</a>.
</p><p>With <a href="/wiki/Quebec_sovereignty_movement" title="Quebec sovereignty movement">Quebec's constitutional status within Canada</a> a rising issue, he shifted to become the <a href="/wiki/Minister_responsible_for_Constitutional_Affairs" title="Minister responsible for Constitutional Affairs">Minister responsible for Constitutional Affairs</a>. The latter position saw him play a leading role in the drafting of the <a href="/wiki/Charlottetown_Accord" title="Charlottetown Accord">Charlottetown Accord</a>, which was decisively rejected in a nationwide referendum and further hurt the standing of the PC party in polls.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="First_retirement_from_Canadian_politics">First retirement from Canadian politics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Joe_Clark&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: First retirement from Canadian politics"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>He retired from politics in 1993, side-stepping the near annihilation of the PC party in the <a href="/wiki/1993_Canadian_federal_election" title="1993 Canadian federal election">1993 election</a> under the leadership of Mulroney's successor <a href="/wiki/Kim_Campbell" title="Kim Campbell">Kim Campbell</a>.
</p><p>Clark was appointed as Special Representative to the <a href="/wiki/Secretary-General_of_the_United_Nations" title="Secretary-General of the United Nations">Secretary-General of the United Nations</a> for <a href="/wiki/Cyprus" title="Cyprus">Cyprus</a> from 1993 to 1996. In 1993, he founded his own consulting firm, Joe Clark and Associates, Ltd., which he still heads. Clark has also served on the boards of directors or advisory boards of several Canadian companies.
</p><p>During the 1993–1994 academic year, Clark served as a Regents' Lecturer in the Canadian Studies Program at the University of California, Berkeley.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1994, he was made a Companion of the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Canada" title="Order of Canada">Order of Canada</a>. Also in 1994, he wrote the book <i>A Nation Too Good to Lose: Renewing the Purpose of Canada</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> This book was also published in a French translation.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/1995_Quebec_referendum" title="1995 Quebec referendum">1995 Quebec referendum</a> saw the federal side win by less than one percent of the vote. It was widely seen as being the failure of the Charlottetown and prior Meech Lake accords that had caused it to be so close.
</p>
<h2><span id="Progressive_Conservative_leadership.2C_1998.E2.80.932003"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Progressive_Conservative_leadership,_1998–2003">Progressive Conservative leadership, 1998–2003</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Joe_Clark&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Progressive Conservative leadership, 1998–2003"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>One of the two PC candidates to survive the <a href="/wiki/1993_Canadian_federal_election" title="1993 Canadian federal election">1993 wipe-out</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jean_Charest" title="Jean Charest">Jean Charest</a>, became leader of the PC party following Campbell's resignation. After leading the party to a modest resurgence in the <a href="/wiki/1997_Canadian_federal_election" title="1997 Canadian federal election">1997 election</a>, winning 20 seats, Charest bowed to tremendous public pressure and left federal politics to become leader of the <a href="/wiki/Quebec_Liberal_Party" title="Quebec Liberal Party">Quebec Liberal Party</a> (unaffiliated with the federal <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada" title="Liberal Party of Canada">Liberals</a>). The party had no obvious candidate to fill Charest's shoes, and turned to Clark once again in 1998.  He was elected by a teleconference of PC members from around the country in which each of the party's <a href="/wiki/Riding_association" title="Riding association">riding associations</a> was allocated 100 points. The points for each riding were then assigned on the basis of each candidate's share of votes within each riding association. Clark defeated <a href="/wiki/Hugh_Segal" title="Hugh Segal">Hugh Segal</a>, free-trade opponent <a href="/wiki/David_Orchard" title="David Orchard">David Orchard</a>, former Manitoba cabinet minister <a href="/wiki/Brian_Pallister" title="Brian Pallister">Brian Pallister</a>, and future <a href="/wiki/Senate_of_Canada" title="Senate of Canada">Senator</a> <a href="/wiki/Michael_Fortier" title="Michael Fortier">Michael Fortier</a> for the leadership of the PC Party.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>It took two years for Clark to return to Parliament.  He was elected for <a href="/wiki/Kings%E2%80%94Hants" title="Kings—Hants">Kings—Hants</a>, Nova Scotia, in a by-election on September 11, 2000, after the incumbent MP, <a href="/wiki/Scott_Brison" title="Scott Brison">Scott Brison</a>, stood down in his favour.  This is common practice when a newly elected party leader does not already have a seat in Parliament.  For the <a href="/wiki/2000_Canadian_federal_election" title="2000 Canadian federal election">general election</a> held two months later, Clark yielded Kings-Hants back to Brison and was elected as the MP for <a href="/wiki/Calgary_Centre" title="Calgary Centre">Calgary Centre</a>, by then deep in the heart of <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Alliance" title="Canadian Alliance">Canadian Alliance</a> territory.
</p><p>Clark ran on his previous experience as Prime Minister and External Affairs Minister. However, he faced a difficult task, with critics and opponents attacking him and the PC Party as a "vote for the past". <a href="/wiki/Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien" title="Jean Chrétien">Jean Chrétien</a>'s governing Liberals were running on their successful economic record, and they were poised to regain the support that they lost in 1997, threatening the PC's 1997 gains in Ontario, Quebec, and the Atlantic provinces. The PC party lost ground in Quebec (due in part to the departure of Jean Charest to provincial politics), which resulted in three members of the PC caucus defecting to join the Liberal Party prior to the election.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> However, Clark was judged by audiences to be the best speaker during the 2000 election debates. The party lost seats to the Liberals, though it managed to hang onto the minimum 12 seats necessary to be recognized in the House of Commons as an official party and therefore qualify for research funding, committee memberships, and minimum speaking privileges. Aside from Clark's Calgary seat (one of only three Alberta seats that did not go to the Canadian Alliance), and one each in <a href="/wiki/Manitoba" title="Manitoba">Manitoba</a> and Quebec, the party's seats were concentrated in Tory bastions in the Atlantic provinces.  Clark continually promoted the idea that the PCs would eventually retake Ontario and form a federal government again. His vision for the party was one that was to the left of the Alliance, but to the right of the Liberals.
</p><p>He soon realized that there was no chance of dislodging the Liberals as long as the centre-right remained split.  However, he wanted a merger on his terms.  He got his chance in 2001, when several dissident Alliance MPs, the most prominent one being Alliance deputy leader and party matriarch <a href="/wiki/Deborah_Grey" title="Deborah Grey">Deborah Grey</a>, left the Alliance caucus. The dissidents felt that Alliance leader <a href="/wiki/Stockwell_Day" title="Stockwell Day">Stockwell Day</a> had not learned from mistakes made in the last election. While some of them rejoined the Alliance later, seven of them, led by <a href="/wiki/Chuck_Strahl" title="Chuck Strahl">Chuck Strahl</a> of British Columbia and including Grey, refused and formed the <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Representative_Caucus" title="Democratic Representative Caucus">Democratic Representative Caucus</a>. The DRC quickly formed a joint caucus with the Tories with Clark as leader.
</p><p>This lasted until 2002, when <a href="/wiki/Stephen_Harper" title="Stephen Harper">Stephen Harper</a> ousted Day as Alliance leader. Harper wanted a closer union with the PCs, but Clark turned the offer down in April 2002, and all but two of the DRC members rejoined the Alliance. One of the two, <a href="/wiki/Inky_Mark" title="Inky Mark">Inky Mark</a>, eventually joined the PCs. Two by-election victories later in 2002 increased the PC caucus to 15 members and fourth place in the Commons.
</p><p>Clark was selected by the media and many parliamentarians for three years in a row to be Canada's most effective opposition leader between 2000 and 2002, pursuing the Liberal government on issues such as <a href="/wiki/Shawinigate" title="Shawinigate">Shawinigate</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Groupaction" title="Groupaction">Groupaction</a> scandal. In his final mandate, Jean Chrétien repeatedly referred to Clark as the <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Official_Opposition_(Canada)" title="Leader of the Official Opposition (Canada)">Leader of the Opposition</a> (Clark was not), much to the chagrin of the Canadian Alliance politicians who occupied the Opposition Leader's chair during the same period. Indeed, Chrétien and Clark had been fellow parliamentarians since the 1970s and they shared a mutual respect despite sitting on opposite benches.
</p><p>Clark's personal popularity grew as, once again, scandal enveloped Chrétien's Liberal government. Clark was widely trusted by Canadians, but this, in his own words, did not translate into more votes and additional seats. Citing this, Clark announced his intention to step down as PC leader on August 6, 2002, at the PC Party's <a href="/wiki/Edmonton" title="Edmonton">Edmonton</a> policy convention. It was expected that a pro-Alliance merger candidate would succeed Clark, but Clark was instead replaced by <a href="/wiki/Peter_MacKay" title="Peter MacKay">Peter MacKay</a> on May 31, 2003. MacKay had signed a controversial deal with Red Tory rival <a href="/wiki/David_Orchard" title="David Orchard">David Orchard</a>, promising not to merge the PC Party with the Alliance. Clark had always encouraged MacKay to keep Orchard and his followers within the PC camp.
</p><p>MacKay immediately reversed his position on seeking a merger, and in 2003, 90% of PC Party delegates voted in favor of a merger with the Canadian Alliance.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Orchard unsuccessfully tried to block the merger and later joined the Liberal Party.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy_of_second_PC_leadership">Legacy of second PC leadership</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Joe_Clark&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Legacy of second PC leadership"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In May 2003, the party finally overtook the <a href="/wiki/New_Democratic_Party_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="New Democratic Party (Canada)">New Democratic Party</a> as the fourth-largest party in the House of Commons, after by-election wins in <a href="/wiki/Newfoundland_and_Labrador" title="Newfoundland and Labrador">Newfoundland and Labrador</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ontario" title="Ontario">Ontario</a>.
</p><p>At the same time, the party was still $10&#160;million in debt from the 2000 election. The PC Party's membership had also dropped from 100,000 in 1998 to 45,000 card carrying PCs in May 2003.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> Clark's leadership of the Progressive Conservatives was also the subject of criticism from many <a href="/wiki/Unite_the_Right_(Canada)" title="Unite the Right (Canada)">United Alternative</a> supporters, who argued that his staunch opposition to a merger with the Reform/Alliance parties helped divide the "conservative" vote during the tenure of <a href="/wiki/Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien" title="Jean Chrétien">Jean Chrétien</a>. Some critics accused Clark of being more interested in helping the interests of his own party and own career than the Canadian conservative movement in general. Others attacked Clark's goal of the PC party regaining its former power as unrealistic.
</p>
<h2><span id="Progressive_Conservative.E2.80.93Canadian_Alliance_merger"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Progressive_Conservative–Canadian_Alliance_merger">Progressive Conservative–Canadian Alliance merger</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Joe_Clark&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Progressive Conservative–Canadian Alliance merger"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>On December 8, 2003, the day that the PC Party and the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Alliance" title="Canadian Alliance">Canadian Alliance</a> were dissolved and the new <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Canada" title="Conservative Party of Canada">Conservative Party of Canada</a> registered, Clark was one of three MPs—the other two were <a href="/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Bachand_(Progressive_Conservative_MP)" title="André Bachand (Progressive Conservative MP)">André Bachand</a> and <a href="/wiki/John_Herron_(New_Brunswick_politician)" title="John Herron (New Brunswick politician)">John Herron</a>—to announce that they would not join the new caucus. MP <a href="/wiki/Scott_Brison" title="Scott Brison">Scott Brison</a> had already joined the Liberals.
</p><p>Clark announced that he would continue to sit for the remainder of the session as an independent Progressive Conservative MP,<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> and retired from <a href="/wiki/Parliament_of_Canada" title="Parliament of Canada">Parliament</a> at the end of the session.
</p><p>Later, Clark openly criticized the new Conservative Party in the run-up to the <a href="/wiki/2004_Canadian_federal_election" title="2004 Canadian federal election">2004 election</a>. He gave a tepid endorsement to the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada" title="Liberal Party of Canada">Liberal Party</a> in the <a href="/wiki/2004_Canadian_federal_election" title="2004 Canadian federal election">2004 election</a>, calling <a href="/wiki/Paul_Martin" title="Paul Martin">Paul Martin</a> "the devil we know".<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> He criticized the new Conservative Party as an "Alliance take-over", and speculated that eastern Canada would not accept the new party or its more socially conservative policies against gay marriage and abortion.
